# Stanford CS329H: ML from Human Preferences | Autumn 2024 — Full Research Document

## Playlist Overview

**Course:** Stanford CS329H: Machine Learning from Human Preferences | Autumn 2024
**Playlist URL:** https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLoROMvodv4rNm525zyAObP4al43WAifZz
**Instructor:** Prof. Sanmi Koyejo (Stanford University, Computer Science)
**Teaching Fellow:** Sang Truong
**Total Videos:** 8
**Total Runtime:** Approximately 9 hours 35 minutes
**Published:** September–October 2024 (uploaded to YouTube September 2025)

**Course Description:**
Machine learning from human preferences investigates mechanisms for capturing human and societal preferences and values in artificial intelligence (AI) systems and applications — for socio-technical applications such as algorithmic fairness and many language and robotics tasks when reward functions are otherwise challenging to specify quantitatively. The course covers foundations of learning from human preferences from first principles, including: inverse reinforcement learning, metric elicitation, reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), choice models, active learning, dueling bandits, social choice, mechanism design, and ethics/value alignment.

1. Introduction — ApF2OenMgfc — 1:16:53

5. Voting (Guest: Colin McIll, Pol.is) — 1QpNZXL35NM — 1:13:22
6. Ethics and Value Alignment (Guest: Dan Weber, Stanford HAI) — -kdR_7dCcyI — 1:10:54
7. Model-based Preference Optimization / Metric Elicitation — 7i6WsIzZaeo — 1:22:40
8. Guest Lecture: Joseph Jay Williams (U Toronto) — Adaptive Experimentation — HFrCySzH9QI — 1:10:30

### Condensed Summary

This opening lecture by Prof. Sanmi Koyejo establishes the intellectual framing and logistics of CS329H. Koyejo introduces himself as a Stanford CS faculty member working on trustworthy ML and AI, with prior work at University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign. The course is in its second year; the first year was purely discussion/lecture, but this iteration introduces homework (60% of grade), group projects, and participation.

The course's central question is how to efficiently and effectively elicit values and preferences from individuals, groups, or societies and embed them in AI models and applications. Koyejo argues this is a timely topic: RLHF ("reinforcement learning from human feedback") has been widely credited with making large language models like ChatGPT work well for users.

Key conceptual distinctions introduced:
- Not all ML is "learning from human preferences" in this course's sense — the course focuses on **explicit, intentional** human signal, not incidental labeling
- **Query efficiency**: how many human interactions are needed to learn a useful model?
- **Interactive vs. batch**: some preference collection is done interactively (online), some offline
- **Rational choice assumption**: the course will largely assume humans have consistent underlying utility functions, though this will be challenged

The course structure covers: (1) Choice models and preference modeling, (2) Model-based techniques (preference learning), (3) Model-free optimization (bandits, DPO), (4) Human values and AI alignment. Guest lectures will include invited speakers from academia and industry.

Major application examples discussed: RLHF for LLMs (the "three-step" pipeline: pretraining → supervised fine-tuning → preference comparison data → RL optimization), exoskeleton calibration using pairwise preferences, recommendation systems, reward hacking (the "boat game" example), and metric elicitation in classification problems. The lecture also surfaces ethical concerns: who provides feedback, what biases get embedded (language models correlate most with "liberal, educated, West Coast, Palo Alto" demographics according to a Pew survey experiment), and the real-world conditions of human labelers who are exposed to traumatic content.

Koyejo notes the reward model controversy: many modern implementations (like DPO) skip an explicit reward model, but he argues reward models have interpretive value for understanding what human preferences the system has learned. The lecture closes by previewing the textbook the course team is writing — the first of its kind on this topic.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**The "three-step RLHF pipeline" as a metaphor for ReloPass authoring:** This lecture's description of the LLM alignment pipeline (pretraining → SFT → preference comparison → RL) maps directly onto ReloPass's corridor authoring pipeline: (1) corridor skeleton (equivalent to pretraining — hardcoded rules), (2) lawyer-written requirement descriptions (equivalent to SFT — expert-generated content), (3) pairwise preference comparison between two versions of a requirement description (equivalent to RLHF comparison data), (4) selecting the better description to keep in the knowledge graph (equivalent to policy optimization). The course provides the theoretical vocabulary for formalizing what ReloPass already does intuitively.

**Query efficiency:** Koyejo's emphasis on query efficiency is directly applicable to ReloPass's lawyer sign-off pipeline. Immigration lawyers are expensive and time-constrained. The question "how many pairwise comparisons do you need to rank requirement descriptions by quality?" is a CS329H question, and active learning methods (covered in later lectures) can minimize the lawyer annotation burden.

**Reward hacking and the "boat game":** The game where an agent maximizes score without completing the course is a precise analogy for a ReloPass failure mode: a requirement description that looks comprehensive and well-formatted but doesn't actually reflect current law. The reward model (lawyer preference signal) must be calibrated to avoid rewarding surface-level quality over substantive accuracy.

**Who provides feedback matters:** The Pew survey finding — that LLMs reflect "Palo Alto" values — is a warning for ReloPass. If only one type of lawyer (e.g., UK corporate immigration specialist) reviews FR→NO corridors, the knowledge graph will reflect their idiosyncratic interpretation. ReloPass needs diverse reviewer profiles per corridor to avoid embedded bias in requirement descriptions.

**Explicit vs. implicit preferences:** Koyejo's distinction between explicit preference elicitation and incidental labeling is useful: ReloPass's lawyer sign-off should be designed as an **explicit** pairwise comparison task ("which version of this requirement description is clearer and more accurate?"), not just a passive approval/rejection, to generate richer preference signal.

### Condensed Summary

This lecture pivots from ML methodology to Human-Computer Interaction (HCI), arguing that machine learning from human preferences is fundamentally a design problem. Koyejo draws on design thinking literature (Norman, Nielsen) to argue that AI tools often fail not because of algorithmic shortcomings but because they are not designed with human needs, capabilities, and mental models in mind.

Central argument: The most profound technologies disappear into everyday use (Weiser's quote). When technology requires instruction manuals or "magic prompts," that's a design failure. AI tools have progressed from code-based interfaces → prompt engineering → conversational interfaces, but still have significant usability gaps.

Key HCI frameworks introduced:
- **Norman Doors analogy**: Bad door design (push vs. pull ambiguity) is analogous to AI tools that require cognitive gymnastics to use. The problem is the design, not the user.
- **Human-Computer Interaction gap**: Technology should extend human capabilities. The lecture traces the evolution from punch cards → command line → GUIs → touchscreens → conversational AI, each step closing the usability gap.
- **Design thinking process**: (1) empathize with users, (2) define the problem, (3) ideate solutions, (4) prototype, (5) test. Applied to AI: understand what the HR generalist, doctor, or student actually needs.
- **Mental models and affordances**: Systems should match users' existing mental models to minimize learning curves.
- **Mixed-initiative systems**: Ideal AI systems allow collaboration where both user and system take active roles, with automated agents taking action only when they have high expected value.

On evaluation: Koyejo distinguishes quantitative evaluation (benchmarks, task completion rates, lines of code) from qualitative evaluation (surveys, think-aloud protocols, understanding "why" failures happen). He notes that benchmark performance and lived experience often diverge for LLMs. Both intrinsic (model-level) and extrinsic (in-context, downstream) evaluation matter. Evaluation itself is a design problem with dimensions of: how (quant/qual), what (model, HCI, end-to-end), who (lay users, domain experts, LLM-as-judge), and when (instant, short-term, longitudinal).

The lecture ends by connecting HCI principles back to preference learning: the tools that ReloPass and similar systems build should be designed so that HR generalists can provide useful feedback, not just technically accurate feedback. The interface through which humans provide preference signals shapes what signal you get.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**The "Norman Door" as a ReloPass risk:** Case Command's timeline output could become a Norman Door — technically correct but unusable. If HR generalists see a 47-step sequence and can't immediately understand which steps they own vs. which are employee-owned vs. which are lawyer-owned, the tool fails its design purpose. The lecture's framework: design from the HR generalist's mental model outward, not from the immigration lawyer's knowledge inward.

**Qualitative vs. quantitative evaluation:** ReloPass should not only track whether users complete relocation cases (quantitative), but also run periodic user interviews to understand where the timeline creates confusion, distrust, or abandonment (qualitative). The gap between benchmark performance and lived experience is directly relevant: a corridor could have 100% legally accurate requirements but fail if the descriptions are written in lawyer-speak that HR generalists don't understand.

**Mixed-initiative design for the authoring pipeline:** The lecture's concept of mixed-initiative systems — where the AI takes action only when it has high expected value — is a design principle for ReloPass's pre-verification pipeline. The system should surface contradiction flags, freshness warnings, and schema errors automatically, but should present them to lawyers in a format that makes the expert's decision easy, not burdensome.

**Evaluation design for lawyer sign-off:** The lecture's four-dimension evaluation framework (how, what, who, when) applies directly: how = pairwise preference comparisons and structured review forms; what = the requirement description quality AND the UX of the review interface; who = immigration lawyers with specific corridor expertise; when = at corridor launch AND periodically as regulations change. Building a good evaluation process for the human-in-the-loop component is itself a design problem.

**HCI for the HR generalist interface:** The lecture's point that prompting is "a somewhat specialized skill" applies to Case Command — if HR generalists need to know the right way to query the system to get useful output, that's a design failure. The amber/red flagging system should be designed so that the meaning is immediately obvious to someone with no immigration knowledge.

### Condensed Summary

This is the core technical lecture on choice models — the mathematical foundations for modeling human preferences. Koyejo presents choice models as a set of tools for predicting choice behavior of individuals or groups within specific contexts, tracing their history from Thurstone's food preference work in the 1920s through McFadden's Nobel Prize-winning work on discrete choice in 2000.

**Key technical content:**

**Bradley-Terry model**: The foundational pairwise preference model. Given two items i and j, the probability that item i is chosen over item j is modeled as: P(i > j) = exp(u_i) / (exp(u_i) + exp(u_j)), where u_i and u_j are latent utility scores. This is equivalent to logistic regression on pairwise comparisons.

**Plackett-Luce model**: Extension to ranked choices over K items. The probability of a particular ranking is the product of conditional probabilities at each stage: P(rank = [i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ]) = ∏ exp(u_{iⱼ}) / Σ_{k≥j} exp(u_{iₖ}).

**Independence of Irrelevant Alternatives (IIA)**: A key axiomatic property of these models. The relative probability of choosing item A over item B is independent of what other items are in the choice set. This is convenient for estimation but can fail in practice (the "red bus/blue bus" problem).

**Random Utility Models (RUM)**: A broader class where utility = deterministic component + random noise. The specific distribution of noise determines the model family: extreme value type I noise gives the logit/Bradley-Terry model; normal noise gives the probit model.

**Rationality assumption**: These models assume preferences are transitive (if A > B and B > C, then A > C). The lecture engages with when this assumption breaks — irrational choices may reflect missing features (add more context and people appear more rational) or fundamental non-transitivity. Koyejo notes this is an active philosophical debate.

**Stated vs. revealed preferences**: A critical distinction. Revealed preferences are observed real-world choices (e.g., which car someone actually buys); stated preferences are hypothetical (survey responses). Stated preferences allow controlled experimental design but may not match real behavior (people state they'll vote one way but vote another). The two types interact with model selection: revealed preferences often have distribution skew, favoring simpler models.

**Applications**: Car choice modeling, transportation route planning, logistics, language model completion ranking (the RLHF comparison step), and recommendation systems.

**Estimation**: Standard ML approach — maximum likelihood estimation, regularization, Bayesian inference. The features Z can describe individuals, items, and alternative options. Modern ML often goes from raw text/embeddings directly to predictions without explicit featurization.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Bradley-Terry for requirement description quality ranking:** This is the most directly applicable technical model for ReloPass's authoring pipeline. When a lawyer reviews two versions of a requirement description and says "version A is clearer," this is a Bradley-Terry observation: P(A > B) = σ(u_A - u_B). Accumulating enough of these pairwise comparisons allows fitting a utility score for each description, enabling automated ranking of requirement quality and selecting the best version for the knowledge graph.

**Plackett-Luce for corridor prioritization:** When multiple lawyers independently rank corridors by "how confident are you in this corridor's requirements?", the Plackett-Luce model can aggregate these ranked signals into a single priority score, identifying which corridors most urgently need lawyer review or updates.

**IIA and its violation in ReloPass:** The IIA assumption means that if lawyers prefer description A over B, that preference shouldn't change when description C is added to the comparison. This might break in practice — if description C is in a different regulatory tradition (e.g., EU-framing vs. UK-framing), its presence might change the reference point and alter A vs. B preferences. ReloPass should be aware of this when designing comparison tasks for multi-corridor review sessions.

**Stated vs. revealed preferences in lawyer review:** Lawyers who review requirement descriptions are providing stated preferences (hypothetical review, no real-world consequence). Their preference might differ from what they'd actually flag in a live case. ReloPass could complement lawyer review with "revealed preference" signals: which requirements do case managers actually look up, re-read, or escalate? Implicit behavioral signals from the platform (time-on-page, number of clicks, escalation rates) provide revealed preferences that can be modeled alongside explicit review signals.

**Rationality assumption for HR generalists:** HR generalists' preferences for requirement presentation may not be transitive. An HR person might prefer: "concise bullet" over "long paragraph", "long paragraph" over "table format", but "table format" over "concise bullet" — i.e., circular preferences. This should be tested during user research. If significant non-transitivity is found, the requirement description format should be standardized rather than preference-learned.

**Random Utility Models and noise in lawyer preferences:** The probit model (normal noise in utility) may better model lawyer preferences than logit (extreme value noise), because lawyer judgments about regulatory accuracy may be more normally distributed around the truth. Worth testing empirically once enough lawyer review data is collected.

### Condensed Summary

This lecture introduces mechanism design — "the science of rule making" — as a tool for eliciting true preferences from strategic actors. The central insight is that when participants have incentives to misrepresent their preferences (be strategic), you need to design the interaction rules such that honest behavior is individually rational.

**Key concepts:**

**Mechanism design setting**: A designer wants to allocate resources (items, attention, tasks) among buyers/agents with private valuations. The challenge: agents will act strategically (bid less than true value, misrepresent preferences) to improve their individual outcomes. The goal: design rules that lead to socially desirable outcomes even under strategic behavior.

**Social choice objective**: The lecture distinguishes revenue maximization (most common in economic settings), welfare maximization (sum of utilities), and other objectives. The designer chooses the objective; mechanism design then determines the rules.

**Posted price mechanisms**: The simplest — set a price, buyer decides to buy or not. Reveals only that the buyer's value exceeds the price. Low information yield.

**First-price auctions**: Buyers bid; highest bidder wins and pays their bid. Incentivizes underbidding ("shade" your bid). Creates strategic behavior.

**Second-price auctions (Vickrey auctions)**: Buyers bid; highest bidder wins but pays the second-highest bid. This has a key property: **truthful bidding is a dominant strategy**. Because you pay what the second-highest bidder was willing to pay, not what you bid, you have no incentive to misrepresent your value. This is the mechanism design ideal.

**Incentive compatibility (IC)**: A mechanism is IC if truthful reporting is a dominant strategy for all agents. Second-price auctions are IC.

**Individual rationality (IR)**: Agents won't participate unless they get non-negative expected utility. Any good mechanism must satisfy IR.

**Myerson's optimal auction theorem**: Among all IC and IR mechanisms, there exists an optimal mechanism that maximizes expected revenue. The solution often involves a "virtual value" transformation of bids.

**Application to digital goods and LLM completions**: The lecture presents a fascinating application — pricing LLM API responses using a second-price auction. Because the value of a completion isn't known until you ask the question, the mechanism must learn a prediction model for completion value using pairwise preferences and thumbs-up/thumbs-down signals. The predicted value replaces the true bid, and second-price auction rules apply to ensure incentive compatibility.

**Hazardous labeling compensation**: Another application — using reverse auctions with pairwise preference signals to determine fair compensation for content moderators exposed to toxic material. The mechanism adapts payments to individual harm sensitivity, providing higher compensation to those for whom the task is more psychologically costly.

**Connections to preference learning**: Auctions ARE preference elicitation. A bid is a stated preference; the mechanism design question is how to structure the elicitation so that revealed and stated preferences align.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Incentive compatibility in lawyer review:** The most important mechanism design insight for ReloPass: if lawyers are asked "which requirement description is better?" without consequence, they may answer quickly and carelessly (strategic behavior to minimize effort). ReloPass should design its review mechanism so that careful, truthful review is individually rational for lawyers. Possible approaches: (1) pay lawyers per reviewed pair rather than per session (creates incentive for completion but not accuracy), (2) include "calibration pairs" with known correct answers and adjust payment/trust scores based on calibration accuracy, (3) make review results visible to the lawyer's firm with attribution (reputational incentive for accuracy).

**Second-price auction logic for corridor prioritization:** ReloPass can apply second-price auction logic to the "which corridor should we expand next?" question. If multiple law firms have different valuations for having a new corridor built (some have many FR→DE clients, others need ES→NO), asking them to "bid" their client need in a second-price auction would reveal true preferences. The corridor that wins gets built; the "winning" firm pays the equivalent of the second-highest need level. This is IC — firms have no incentive to over-state their need.

**Mechanism design for the pre-verification pipeline:** The contradiction check and freshness check in the pre-verification pipeline should be designed as IC mechanisms. If a lawyer can "pass" a requirement by stating it's fresh without actually verifying, they will if the cost of verification is high. Design the pipeline so that false verification is caught (calibration checks) and has consequences (reduced review trust score), making truthful verification individually rational.

**Virtual values and corridor pricing:** Myerson's virtual value concept is applicable when ReloPass considers pricing corridor access. Some SMEs have very high value for a specific corridor (only one that matters for them) but won't reveal it. A mechanism with virtual value pricing ensures that the pricing structure extracts appropriate value without driving away lower-value users.

**Adaptive payment for lawyer harm:** The hazardous labeling application maps precisely to ReloPass's lawyer review of sensitive requirements (e.g., reviewing descriptions of denied visa categories, deportation triggers, or sanction lists). If reviewing certain requirements is more psychologically taxing or legally risky (e.g., reviewing sanctioned-individual requirements for a lawyer at risk of professional liability), adaptive compensation based on revealed harm sensitivity could ensure adequate review coverage.

### Condensed Summary

This guest lecture features Colin McIll, President of the Pol.is nonprofit (formerly CEO when it was a for-profit, 2012–2016), discussing the platform's use of statistical methods — principally PCA and k-means clustering — to surface consensus and disagreement patterns in large-scale public deliberation.

**Pol.is mechanics**: Participants submit statements and vote (agree/disagree/pass) on statements submitted by others. This creates a sparse vote matrix where each row is a participant (their voting record) and each column is a submitted statement. PCA + k-means clustering on this matrix reveals opinion clusters — groups of people who vote similarly — and identifies which statements differentiate groups ("dividing statements") vs. which statements gain cross-group agreement ("consensus statements").

**The key metric**: Consensus amid disagreement. If a statement gets high agreement from people who otherwise vote very differently, that's a signal of genuine broad consensus — more meaningful than simple majority. This was implemented and became the methodological foundation for Twitter/X's Community Notes.

**Real-world deployments**: National governments of UK, Finland, Singapore, Taiwan, Amsterdam/Netherlands use Pol.is. The most-studied case is Taiwan's use for Uber regulation — when Uber sent all their drivers to game the conversation, the clustering algorithm naturally revealed the Uber driver group as a cluster rather than suppressing other views. The platform was somewhat robust because adding a large homogeneous group just creates a larger cluster, not a distortion of others.

**Adversarial robustness and LLM threats**: The lecture discusses the emerging risk of LLM-generated synthetic participants creating fake clusters that don't represent real opinion groups. The current mitigation path is better identity verification (ZK-proof based anonymous-but-verified identity systems), not algorithmic changes. The voting surface area is small (agree/disagree/pass), limiting attack vectors.

**LLM integration opportunities**: Pol.is is exploring LLMs for: (1) generating diverse statement options from seed prompts (AB Scribe tool — use LLMs to generate message variations), (2) semantic transfer — predicting how a participant would vote on unseen statements based on their voting history and statement semantics, (3) cluster interpretation — using LLMs to summarize what a cluster "believes."

**Connections to RLHF**: Koyejo draws explicit connections — the Pol.is voting matrix is a revealed preference dataset. The clusters found by k-means on PCA-reduced voting data are preference groups. The "consensus amid disagreement" metric is essentially a social choice function operating over pairwise preferences.

**Key epistemological debate in the lecture**: A student challenges whether the algorithm is truly "emergent" or whether the algorithmic choices (how many clusters, what PCA transformation) are imposing structure. McIll acknowledges this and offers the path of open API access so data scientists can re-analyze the raw matrix. The debate mirrors a deep question in preference learning: do our models find structure or impose it?

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Pol.is-style consensus for corridor validation:** ReloPass could use a Pol.is-inspired approach for validating corridor requirements with multiple lawyers from different jurisdictions. Present statements like "The employee must register with the local tax authority within 30 days of arrival in Norway" and ask lawyers to agree/disagree. PCA + clustering on the response matrix would reveal: (1) consensus requirements (all lawyers agree), (2) disputed requirements (significant disagreement — needs further research), (3) jurisdictionally-specific views (French lawyers vs. Norwegian lawyers cluster differently on the same requirement). This is a scalable alternative to one-on-one lawyer consultations.

**Community Notes logic for requirement flagging:** The "consensus amid disagreement" metric is directly applicable to ReloPass's contradiction detection. If lawyers from France AND Norway AND a UK international law firm all agree that a requirement is outdated, that cross-cluster agreement is a high-confidence signal to trigger a freshness check — stronger than if only one lawyer flagged it. Implementing a weighted consensus score (Bradley-Terry × cross-cluster agreement) would prioritize which requirements most urgently need re-verification.

**Adversarial robustness of the review pipeline:** The Pol.is discussion about LLM-generated fake participants is analogous to a ReloPass risk: a law firm with a conflict of interest could submit systematically favorable reviews of requirements that benefit their clients (e.g., making a cumbersome requirement look easy, reducing HR generalist anxiety about a path that the firm profits from). ReloPass should design review mechanisms with identity verification (only verified immigration lawyers with confirmed jurisdictional credentials can review specific corridors) and detect suspiciously homogeneous review patterns.

**Sparse voting matrix as preference data:** The Pol.is sparse matrix structure — where most participants haven't voted on most statements — is the same structure as ReloPass's partial lawyer review data. Not every lawyer reviews every requirement. PCA on the sparse review matrix (using matrix factorization with missing values) can reveal latent quality dimensions of requirements and identify which requirements have high uncertainty (few reviews) vs. high confidence (many convergent reviews).

**Scale vs. depth tradeoff:** Pol.is deliberately uses low-cognitive-cost voting (agree/disagree/pass on atomic statements) to scale participation. ReloPass should apply this to its validation pipeline: instead of asking lawyers to do comprehensive corridor reviews (high effort, low participation), ask them to vote on individual requirements (low effort, high participation). Accumulate many weak signals rather than few strong ones.

### Condensed Summary

This guest lecture by philosopher-in-residence Dan Weber addresses the ethics of value alignment — specifically the philosophical complexity of getting AI to do what we "really" want, and what frameworks can help structure thinking about this problem.

**The paperclip maximizer thought experiment (Bostrom)**: An AI given the goal of "maximize paperclip production" eventually converts all available matter into paperclips. The lesson: explicitly stated goals often diverge dramatically from intended goals because human instructions rely on implicit background assumptions that are hard to formalize. The problem isn't skill — even well-specified instructions can fail to capture everything we actually want.

**Three framings of value alignment:**
1. **Intention alignment**: AI should derive what we truly intend from under-specified instructions. Requires a complete model of human language, culture, and institutions. Weber notes this requires "a complete model of human language and interaction" — and asks how well this has aged given LLM progress (mixed verdict).
2. **Preference alignment**: AI should learn what we prefer through revealed or stated preference data. But preferences are unstable, context-dependent, and often self-contradictory. Weber discusses Kahneman's "experiencing self vs. remembering self" distinction — preferences stated after an experience can differ from in-the-moment preferences.
3. **Values alignment**: AI should align with our values, which are more stable than preferences but require normative ethical frameworks to specify. Weber covers major frameworks: **consequentialism** (maximize good outcomes — but measurement is contested), **deontology** (follow rules/duties regardless of consequences — but rule specification has the same under-specification problem), **virtue ethics** (cultivate the character traits of a good person — but requires a theory of what "good person" means).

**The measurement problem**: Even if we agree on the goal (e.g., "maximize wellbeing"), measuring it is contested. GDP, hedonic scales, preference satisfaction, capabilities approaches all measure different things. Which measure we optimize shapes which world we build.

**Constitutional AI and rule-based value alignment**: Weber discusses approaches like Anthropic's Constitutional AI — giving the AI explicit rules/principles to follow — as an attempt at deontological alignment. The upside: transparent, auditable rules. The downside: Goodhart's Law — the model learns to satisfy the rule specification, not the underlying intent. "When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure."

**Aggregation and whose values**: Even if we solve individual preference elicitation, aggregating across diverse populations raises social choice problems (Arrow's impossibility). Whose values should the AI align with? The majority's? The most affected? The most vulnerable? Weber doesn't resolve this but frames it as a necessary design decision, not a technical one.

**Ethical review for CS329H projects (ESR)**: The lecture closes with guidance on Ethics, Safety, and Responsibility statements required for course projects — identifying potential harms, populations affected, mitigation strategies.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Intention alignment for requirement descriptions:** The paperclip maximizer is precisely the risk for ReloPass if requirements are written to satisfy search queries rather than to give HR generalists actionable guidance. A requirement description that is technically correct ("Employee must register with Skattetaten") but operationally useless ("what is Skattetaten? Where? Which form? What's the deadline?") satisfies the stated requirement but not the intent. Weber's framework: what is the background assumption that makes this useful? Write it down explicitly.

**Constitutional AI for corridor quality:** Anthropic's Constitutional AI approach — giving an LLM a set of principles it must evaluate against — could be applied to ReloPass's requirement authoring pipeline. A "requirement constitution" could include rules like: (1) Every requirement must specify a responsible party, (2) Every deadline must be expressed in days from a named trigger event, (3) No requirement may use unexplained acronyms, (4) Every requirement must link to a primary legal source. An LLM authoring assistant constrained by these rules would produce more consistently useful output than unconstrained generation.

**Preference vs. values alignment for HR generalists:** HR generalists may state preferences (short requirements, bullet points, no legal jargon) that conflict with their underlying values (comprehensive coverage, legal defensibility). ReloPass should not simply optimize for stated preferences (which might lead to dangerously oversimplified requirements) but for stated values ("I want to be confident that I haven't missed anything") which requires completeness even at the cost of complexity. This distinction — preference vs. value alignment — should shape how user research is conducted and how feedback is weighted.

**Goodhart's Law for reward models:** Weber's Goodhart warning applies directly to ReloPass's reward model design. If lawyers are asked to rate requirements on "clarity" and the system optimizes for clarity scores, it will produce very clear requirements that may sacrifice legal precision. The reward model should include multiple dimensions: clarity AND legal accuracy AND completeness AND actionability, with weights that reflect actual HR generalist outcomes (successful relocations, no compliance failures), not just surface-level preferences.

**Whose preferences to align with:** ReloPass has multiple stakeholders — HR generalists, immigration lawyers, employees being relocated, corporate compliance officers, government immigration authorities. Weber's framework: this is a design decision, not a technical one. ReloPass's explicit decision is to optimize for the HR generalist's workflow, treating lawyers as quality validators and employees as the ultimate beneficiaries. This needs to be stated explicitly and revisited as the user base grows.

**Ethics, Safety, and Responsibility for ReloPass:** Applying the ESR framework: (1) Potential harms — incorrect requirement flagging causing employees to miss immigration deadlines → visa revocations; (2) Populations affected — employees on work visas, their families, SME companies with limited legal resources; (3) Mitigation — deterministic rule engine (no hallucination), lawyer sign-off before corridor goes live, amber/red flags that escalate rather than decide, explicit "not legal advice" disclaimers.

### Condensed Summary

This lecture presents Koyejo's own research program on **metric elicitation** — a specific and technically rich version of preference learning where the goal is to elicit the right *evaluation metric* for a classification problem from domain experts using pairwise preference queries. The lecture connects this to active learning (from the previous lecture), mechanism design (upcoming), and the general RLHF pipeline.

**The core problem — cost-sensitive classification**: When building a classifier (e.g., cancer diagnosis, credit approval), different types of errors have different real-world costs. A false positive (diagnosing cancer in a healthy patient) has different consequences than a false negative (missing cancer in a sick patient). The "right" metric is a weighted combination of false positive rate and false negative rate, but the weights are not known a priori — they must be elicited from domain experts (oncologists, ethicists, policy makers).

**Confusion space geometry**: All possible classifiers (varying a decision threshold or mixing two classifiers) trace out a convex set in the (false positive rate, false negative rate) space — the ROC curve. A linear metric (weighted combination of FPR and FNR) corresponds to a linear hyperplane in this confusion space. Finding the "right" metric means finding the hyperplane that passes through the confusion matrix point that experts prefer.

**Binary search on the confusion space**: Koyejo's key insight — because the confusion space is convex and the metric is linear, you can use a binary search (bisection) algorithm to find the expert-preferred operating point with O(log(1/ε)) queries. Each query asks the expert: "Given that you're comparing these two classifiers' outcomes, which would you prefer?" The binary search navigates toward the preferred operating point efficiently.

**Query complexity bounds**: The lecture provides explicit bounds on how many expert queries are needed to identify the metric to within ε-accuracy. This is the "active learning for preferences" result — you need O(log(1/ε)) queries, not O(1/ε²) as you would with random sampling. For practical expert elicitation (expensive lawyers, doctors), this logarithmic savings is decisive.

**Noise-robust version**: The probabilistic bisection algorithm handles noisy expert responses (experts sometimes give inconsistent answers). It maintains a prior distribution over possible threshold points and updates based on each pairwise response, similar to Thompson sampling. This gives probabilistic convergence guarantees even with noisy labels.

**Connections to active learning**: The metric elicitation problem IS an active learning problem: which query should I ask next to learn the expert's metric most efficiently? The answer from active learning theory: ask the query that maximally reduces entropy about the unknown metric parameters.

**Connection to pre-training + preference tuning metaphor**: Koyejo frames the pipeline as: (1) pre-train a model on data (learn the conditional probability distribution), (2) preference-tune the decision rule using expert feedback on pairwise comparisons of classifier outputs. This directly mirrors the RLHF pipeline for LLMs.

**Inverse decision theory application**: A second paper presented applies similar ideas at the individual example level (not population level), asking experts about specific cases rather than population-level error rates. More cognitively accessible but requires a different geometric analysis.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Metric elicitation for corridor quality assessment:** Koyejo's framework is directly applicable to ReloPass's lawyer review pipeline. The "metric" to be elicited is: what is the right weighting between completeness and clarity when evaluating a requirement description? Present lawyers with pairs of requirement descriptions: one highly complete but verbose, one clear but potentially missing edge cases. The pairwise choices trace out the lawyer's implicit metric (their preferred operating point on the completeness-clarity tradeoff curve). Binary search finds this point in O(log(1/ε)) queries.

**ROC curve analogy for requirement descriptions:** Just as all classifiers lie on a ROC curve in (FPR, FNR) space, all requirement descriptions lie on a Pareto frontier in (completeness, clarity) space. A requirement at one extreme is a complete legal brief; at the other extreme, it's a one-sentence summary. The corridor knowledge graph should target operating points on this frontier that match the HR generalist's needs — more toward clarity for standard corridors, more toward completeness for complex or high-risk moves.

**Active query design for lawyer sign-off:** Instead of asking lawyers to review all requirements in a corridor (expensive, low compliance), use active learning to prioritize: which requirement pairs, when compared, would most reduce uncertainty about the corridor's quality? This is directly the metric elicitation setting. Start with requirements that have high uncertainty (recently changed, based on single source, flagged by freshness check) and ask pairwise comparisons that resolve the most uncertainty first.

**Binary search for threshold calibration:** Case Command's amber/red flagging uses lead-time thresholds (e.g., "amber if < 30 days, red if < 14 days"). These thresholds should be calibrated to HR generalist risk tolerance, not arbitrary values. Use pairwise queries to elicit thresholds: "Given a case where the employee needs a work permit and there are 25 days until the start date, should this be amber or red?" Binary search on the timeline space finds the correct threshold in O(log(n)) queries.

**Noise-robust elicitation for inconsistent lawyers:** Different lawyers reviewing the same requirement pair may give different answers (stated preference vs. true preference, jurisdictional differences). The probabilistic bisection algorithm is robust to this noise. ReloPass should implement a Bayesian version of the review aggregation that maintains uncertainty about each requirement's quality score, not a simple average of binary approve/reject signals.

**Pre-training + preference tuning for requirement authoring:** The lecture's framing — pre-train on data, then preference-tune the decision rule — applies to ReloPass's LLM-assisted authoring (future roadmap). Pre-train on all existing immigration law documents; then preference-tune using lawyer pairwise comparisons of generated requirement descriptions. The confusion space intuition: the "classifier" is the LLM's choice of description style, and the "operating point" is the style that best matches the lawyer's (and ultimately the HR generalist's) preference.

### Condensed Summary

Joseph Jay Williams presents his research program on **adaptive experimentation for intelligent interventions** — using multi-armed bandit algorithms, Thompson sampling, and related techniques to design behavioral interventions (text messages, emails, explanations) that learn and improve over time. The lecture bridges human preferences, online learning, and real-world deployment.

**The core vision**: Transform everyday technology touch points (emails, text messages, website explanations) into "intelligent interventions" — A/B tests that are adaptive, personalized, and continuously improving. The ADComp (Adaptive Component) framework takes any user interface element and turns it into a micro-experiment.

**Thompson sampling and Beta distributions**: The fundamental mechanism. When comparing option A vs. option B, rather than 50/50 random assignment (classical A/B), maintain a beta distribution over "what is the probability that A is better than B?" As evidence accumulates, assign people to options proportionally to their posterior probability of being the best. This is Thompson sampling — a Bayesian bandit algorithm. Key properties: (1) exploration decreases as confidence increases, (2) no need to pre-specify a stopping criterion, (3) sample-efficient compared to fixed A/B.

**The probabilistic assignment insight**: Williams argues that humans operate as binary creatures in a probabilistic world — we either do something 100% of the time (our best guess) or 50/50 (A/B test). The correct approach is weighted randomization proportional to posterior belief. If you're 70% sure option A is better, assign 70% of users to A, not 100%.

**Qualitative A/B testing (AB Scribe)**: Williams introduces the idea of applying A/B testing logic without quantitative data — using LLMs to generate multiple versions of content (emails, messages) and using human judgment to evaluate which is likely to be better. This "qualitative AB comparison" is a thought experiment mode for practitioners without large user bases.

**Statistical minorities problem**: A critical concern — the best intervention on average can actively harm subgroups. An email that gets more homework started on time for the average student might demotivate a specific cultural group. Williams argues that adaptive experimentation should explicitly detect statistical minorities (subgroups who respond atypically) and route them to personalized treatments.

**Contextual bandits for personalization**: The lecture transitions from stateless bandits (same arm for everyone) to contextual bandits (arm selection depends on context: user demographics, time of day, platform, etc.). This is necessary for personalization — the same email might work for undergraduate students but fail for graduate students.

**LLM integration**: Williams is excited about LLMs for: (1) generating diverse candidate interventions (50 email variants instead of 2), (2) predicting which variants might work for which contexts without running experiments, (3) semantic clustering of successful vs. failed interventions to extract generalizable principles.

**Power analysis and small samples**: Addresses the concern that most practitioners don't have 100,000 users. In small-sample settings, adaptive experimentation with Thompson sampling still outperforms fixed A/B by concentrating trials on promising options faster. For very small n, qualitative A/B (thought experiments with LLM-generated variants) provides value.

**Application domains covered**: Education (email timing for homework completion), health (exercise and stress management), behavioral economics, and general web/app engagement.

### ReloPass Application Notes

**Thompson sampling for corridor expansion decisions:** ReloPass's biggest strategic bandit problem: which corridor should we build next? Each corridor is an "arm." The "reward" is a combination of: number of active cases in that corridor, HR generalist time saved per case, lawyer review cost. Thompson sampling with beta-distribution priors (based on sales conversations, search data, and user survey signals) can allocate ReloPass's corridor development budget adaptively — building the corridor with the highest posterior expected value, updating as corridors go live and actual usage data comes in.

**Adaptive experimentation for requirement description format:** Within Case Command's UI, the format of how requirements are displayed (bullet points vs. timeline vs. table vs. narrative) can be A/B tested using Thompson sampling. Each HR generalist interaction with a requirement becomes a data point: did they click to expand? Did they escalate to a lawyer? Did they complete the step without errors? These implicit signals are the reward. Thompson sampling adaptively routes new users to the display format that performs best for their corridor/role/company size.

**ADComp framework for the lawyer review interface:** The lawyer review interface (where lawyers provide pairwise comparisons of requirement descriptions) should itself be an ADComp — an adaptive component that tests different comparison formats, question framings, and presentation modes. Does showing the legal source alongside the description improve review quality? Does a structured rubric increase inter-rater agreement? Adaptive experimentation answers these questions with real usage data.

**Qualitative A/B for new corridor content without user data:** When a new corridor (e.g., DE→PL) is being built and there are no live users yet, use qualitative AB testing. Generate 10 versions of a requirement description using an LLM, have one lawyer evaluate which they'd prefer to review, and use that to select the starting point. This is Williams's "thought experiment" mode — zero data but structured thinking.

**Statistical minorities in HR generalist populations:** ReloPass serves HR generalists at SMEs from 200–10,000 employees. A requirement description format that works for a 5,000-person tech company's HR team may confuse a 200-person manufacturing firm's part-time HR generalist. Statistical minorities detection: segment by company size, sector, and HR sophistication. Flag when a format change harms a subgroup even if it helps the average. Use contextual bandits (not stateless bandits) to route different user types to their optimal format.

**LLM-assisted requirement generation as multi-arm bandit:** When ReloPass eventually uses an LLM to draft initial requirement descriptions, the generation process can be framed as a bandit problem: generate K candidate descriptions (K=10–20), have one lawyer do a quick ranking (5 minutes), use the ranking as reward signal, and update the LLM's generation policy toward the description style that lawyers consistently prefer. Williams's framework makes this principled and sample-efficient.

**Power analysis for small lawyer cohorts:** ReloPass will have a small number of lawyers per corridor (maybe 3–10 at launch). Williams's discussion of small-sample adaptive experimentation is directly applicable. For the FR→NO corridor, even with 5 reviewers, Thompson sampling on pairwise comparisons can identify the best requirement description format within ~20 review sessions — much faster than a fixed A/B with significance testing.

## Course-Level Summary

### Overall Learning Arc

CS329H: Machine Learning from Human Preferences (Autumn 2024) traces a coherent intellectual journey from the theoretical foundations of preference modeling through to practical applications in value alignment and adaptive intervention design.

**Module 1 — Foundations (Videos 1–3):** The course opens by establishing the core question: how do we efficiently and intentionally elicit human preferences for AI systems? The Introduction frames the field and motivates the RLHF pipeline. Human-centered Design grounds the technical work in HCI principles, arguing that preference learning is ultimately a user experience problem — the tool must be usable for the human providing the signal. Preference Models (Choice Models) provides the technical bedrock: Bradley-Terry, Plackett-Luce, Random Utility Models, stated vs. revealed preferences, and the rationality assumption — with its limitations.

**Module 2 — Strategic and Social Settings (Videos 4–5):** Mechanism Design introduces game theory and the incentive compatibility problem: how do you elicit true preferences from strategic actors? The Vickrey second-price auction is the paradigm example of a truthful mechanism. The Voting lecture (Colin McIll/Pol.is) scales this to population-level preference aggregation using dimensionality reduction (PCA + k-means) to find genuine consensus amid disagreement — the foundation of Community Notes.

**Module 3 — Ethics and Values (Video 6):** The Ethics lecture (Dan Weber) confronts the philosophical complexity: preference alignment, intention alignment, and values alignment are distinct and often in tension. Constitutional AI is a deontological approach; reward modeling is a consequentialist approach. Goodhart's Law applies to all.

**Module 4 — Technical Depth and Applications (Videos 7–8):** Metric Elicitation deepens the technical machinery — using binary search on convex confusion spaces to find the right evaluation metric in O(log(1/ε)) queries. The final guest lecture (Joseph Jay Williams) applies adaptive experimentation (Thompson sampling, contextual bandits) to real-world behavioral interventions, showing how preference learning closes the loop between design and deployment.

**Unifying themes:**
- Query efficiency: how do we minimize human annotation burden?
- Rationality and its limits: utility models are approximations; where do they break?
- Strategic behavior: people will game any elicitation system if they have incentive to do so
- Aggregation: moving from individual to group preferences introduces Arrow's impossibility and social choice problems
- Ethical stakes: who provides feedback, whose values get encoded, and what are the downstream harms?

---

### Top 10 Actionable Insights

1. **Pairwise comparisons are the gold standard for eliciting human preferences.** Asking "which is better — A or B?" is more reliable than absolute ratings ("rate this 1–5") because it eliminates individual scale biases. Bradley-Terry models can aggregate thousands of pairwise comparisons into reliable utility scores. *Apply everywhere human evaluation is needed.*

2. **Query efficiency is a first-order concern when human time is expensive.** Active learning methods (binary search on convex spaces, Thompson sampling, Bayesian optimization) can reduce annotation cost by orders of magnitude compared to random sampling. The metric elicitation result — O(log(1/ε)) queries — is the theoretical minimum for finding a preferred operating point in a convex space. *Design annotation pipelines with query efficiency as a primary objective, not an afterthought.*

3. **Incentive compatibility is essential for any human-in-the-loop system.** If reviewers, raters, or annotators can benefit from misrepresentation, they will. Second-price auction logic — design the mechanism so truthful reporting is individually rational — is the foundational insight. *Include calibration checks, reputation systems, or economic incentives that make honest review the individually optimal strategy.*

4. **The stated vs. revealed preference gap is large and often ignored.** What people say they prefer in surveys diverges systematically from what they do in practice. Election polling, LLM completion preferences, and medical decision-making all show this gap. *Supplement stated preference data (surveys, pairwise comparisons) with behavioral signals (click rates, task completion, escalation rates) as revealed preference proxies.*

5. **Consensus amid disagreement is more informative than majority preference.** The Pol.is/Community Notes insight: if people who disagree on everything else all agree on something, that agreement is a much stronger signal than majority opinion in a homogeneous group. *In multi-stakeholder validation systems, cross-group consensus is the highest-quality signal for establishing ground truth.*

6. **Reward models are hackable; Goodhart's Law always applies.** Systems optimized for a proxy metric (helpfulness ratings, lawyer approval rates, user engagement) will find ways to game it. The "boat game" example — an agent that exploits reward function structure rather than completing the intended task — is the canonical failure mode. *Design reward functions with multiple dimensions, include calibration tasks with known answers, and monitor for reward hacking patterns.*

7. **Value alignment requires choosing between consequentialism, deontology, and virtue ethics — not avoiding the choice.** Constitutional AI is deontological; RLHF is consequentialist; human-centered design is closest to virtue ethics. Each has failure modes. The choice of ethical framework is a design decision that must be made explicitly. *State which ethical framework your system implements and what its known failure modes are.*

8. **Thompson sampling dominates fixed A/B testing in most practical settings.** The binary (50/50) vs. one-arm (100% best guess) world is a false dichotomy. Bayesian weighted randomization proportional to posterior beliefs is sample-efficient, adaptive, and avoids both exploration waste and exploitation of incorrect beliefs. *Replace fixed A/B testing with Thompson sampling for any iterative content or feature optimization.*

9. **Statistical minorities are harmed by average-optimized systems.** The best policy on average actively harms subgroups who respond atypically. Detecting statistical minorities requires explicit monitoring: segment by context variables, test for heterogeneous treatment effects, route different user types to personalized treatments via contextual bandits. *Build statistical minority detection into every A/B and adaptive experimentation system from the start.*

10. **Human-centered design is a prerequisite, not an afterthought, for preference learning systems to work.** If the interface through which humans provide preference signals is poorly designed (cognitively burdensome, ambiguous, inconsistent), the signal quality degrades. Norman's "bad door" insight applies to review interfaces, annotation tools, and any human-in-the-loop component. *Invest in UX research and iterative design for human feedback interfaces before scaling annotation volume.*

---

### ReloPass Strategic Applications (Detailed)

**Executive Summary**

CS329H provides ReloPass with a comprehensive theoretical vocabulary and toolkit for formalizing and improving its human-in-the-loop pipeline. The most important applications cluster around three themes: (1) formalizing the lawyer sign-off process as a principled preference learning system, (2) using active learning and bandit algorithms to minimize expensive human annotation, and (3) applying mechanism design principles to ensure that strategic behavior by reviewers doesn't corrupt the knowledge graph.

---

**Application 1: Formalizing the Lawyer Sign-Off Pipeline as RLHF**

The course's description of the RLHF pipeline for LLMs (pretraining → SFT → preference comparison data → RL optimization) maps directly onto ReloPass's corridor authoring pipeline:

- **Pretraining** ↔ ReloPass's deterministic rule engine — the hardcoded immigration law structure
- **Supervised Fine-Tuning** ↔ Lawyer-authored requirement descriptions — expert content generation
- **Preference Comparison Data** ↔ Pairwise lawyer comparisons of requirement description versions
- **Policy Optimization** ↔ Selecting the preferred description for the knowledge graph

Currently, this mapping is implicit and informal. CS329H provides the technical tools to formalize it:

1. **Bradley-Terry model** (Video 3): When a lawyer says "version A of this requirement is clearer than version B," this is a Bradley-Terry observation. Accumulating these observations allows fitting utility scores for description styles, enabling automated quality ranking.

2. **Active learning / binary search** (Video 7): When multiple versions of a requirement exist (e.g., after a regulatory change), active binary search on the "completeness vs. clarity" Pareto frontier finds the lawyer-preferred version in O(log(1/ε)) pairwise comparisons — far fewer than exhaustive review.

3. **Reward model** (Video 1): The accumulated lawyer preferences can be used to train a reward model that predicts lawyer preference for new requirement descriptions without requiring lawyer review for every new text. This is the path toward scalable, automated quality assessment.

**Specific implementation roadmap:**
- Phase 1: Instrument the lawyer review interface to capture pairwise comparisons (not just approve/reject) — 2 months
- Phase 2: Fit a Bradley-Terry model on accumulated comparison data to estimate requirement quality scores — 1 month
- Phase 3: Use quality scores to prioritize which requirements need human review vs. which can be auto-approved — ongoing

---

**Application 2: Incentive-Compatible Lawyer Review Design (Mechanism Design)**

The mechanism design lecture (Video 4) identifies the key risk: lawyers who review requirements have incentives to minimize effort (approve quickly) rather than review carefully. This is a strategic behavior problem.

**Applying second-price auction logic:**
- Include "calibration requirements" — requirement descriptions with known correct and incorrect versions — in every review session. Lawyers who approve the incorrect version get a lower "review trust score."
- Weight lawyer review signals by trust score when aggregating pairwise comparisons into the Bradley-Terry utility estimate.
- Make trust scores visible to the lawyer's firm (reputational incentive for accuracy).
- Pay lawyers based on calibrated review quality, not review volume.

**Incentive compatibility property:** This mechanism makes careful, truthful review individually rational for lawyers: the cost of approving a bad requirement (lower trust score, reduced future compensation, reputational risk) outweighs the effort savings from quick approval.

**Individual rationality property:** Lawyers won't participate unless the review task is worthwhile. Design the minimum viable review session (15–20 minutes, no more than 10 pairwise comparisons per session) to ensure participation is rational even for busy practitioners.

---

**Application 3: Adaptive Corridor Expansion Using Thompson Sampling**

The core strategic question for ReloPass: which corridor should we build next? This is a multi-armed bandit problem (Video 8):

- Each corridor is an "arm" (e.g., FR→NO, ES→IE, DE→PL, UK→FR, etc.)
- The "reward" for building a corridor = expected revenue × probability of customer adoption × HR generalist time saved
- Prior on reward = estimated from sales pipeline signals, website search data, customer interviews

**Thompson sampling implementation:**
- Maintain a Beta(α, β) distribution over each corridor's expected reward
- When allocating development capacity, sample from each corridor's posterior and assign resources to the highest sample
- After each corridor goes live, update α and β based on actual revenue and usage
- This concentrates effort on corridors with the highest posterior expected value while continuing to explore less-certain options

**Key advantage over the current approach:** Rather than gut-feel prioritization based on loudest customer voice, Thompson sampling provides a principled, updatable decision framework that improves as data accumulates.

---

**Application 4: Statistical Minority Detection for Corridor Users**

Williams's statistical minorities warning (Video 8) applies to ReloPass: a requirement display format that works for a large-company HR department (sophisticated users, multiple HR staff, access to legal counsel) may fail for a small-company HR generalist (single person, no legal background, time-pressured).

**Implementation:**
- Segment Case Command users by: company size, sector, HR team size, self-reported immigration familiarity
- Track outcomes by segment: did the user complete the step? Did they escalate to a lawyer? Did they make an error?
- When a format change or new requirement description improves average performance but harms a subgroup, flag it as a statistical minority issue
- Use contextual bandits (not stateless) to route different user segments to their optimal requirement presentation format

---

**Application 5: Consensus-Based Validation (Pol.is-Inspired)**

For high-stakes requirements (those with amber or red flags, or those based on single sources), convene a multi-lawyer validation panel using Pol.is-style voting:

- Present the requirement as a statement: "The employee must register with the Norwegian Tax Administration (Skatteetaten) within 8 days of arriving in Norway."
- Ask lawyers from France, Norway, and an independent international law firm to agree/disagree
- Apply PCA + k-means on the response matrix to identify: (1) consensus statements (all lawyers agree), (2) disputed statements (significant disagreement), (3) jurisdictionally-specific views (French lawyers vs. Norwegian lawyers)
- Only include requirements in the live corridor that achieve cross-cluster consensus among lawyers from both origin and destination jurisdictions

This implements the "consensus amid disagreement" insight from the Voting lecture — cross-jurisdictional agreement is a stronger signal than single-jurisdiction approval.

---

**Application 6: Constitutional AI for Requirement Description Authoring**

The Ethics lecture (Video 6) introduces Constitutional AI as a deontological approach to value alignment — giving the AI explicit principles to evaluate against. For ReloPass's LLM-assisted requirement authoring (future roadmap):

**Requirement Constitution (draft rules):**
1. Every requirement must specify exactly one responsible party (employee, employer, or immigration lawyer)
2. Every deadline must be expressed in calendar days from a named trigger event (arrival date, visa issuance, contract signature)
3. No requirement may use unexplained acronyms without a parenthetical definition on first use
4. Every requirement must link to at least one primary legal source (legislation, ministry website, or official guidance)
5. Requirements must not speculate about future regulatory changes
6. Requirements affecting the employee personally must include a plain-language explanation of consequences of non-compliance

An LLM constrained by these rules, with lawyer pairwise preference feedback to calibrate which rules matter most in which contexts, would produce consistently high-quality requirement descriptions. The rules themselves can be preference-tuned over time as more lawyer feedback accumulates.

---

**Application 7: Active Learning for the Pre-Verification Pipeline**

ReloPass's pre-verification pipeline (source check, freshness check, schema check, contradiction check) generates signals about which requirements may be outdated or incorrect. But lawyer verification time is limited. Active learning (Video 7) determines which requirements to prioritize for verification:

**Active learning selection criteria:**
1. **Uncertainty**: requirements with high uncertainty in the Bradley-Terry utility score (few reviews, inconsistent reviewer judgments) → prioritize for lawyer review
2. **User impact**: requirements on the critical path for high-volume corridors → higher priority than rarely-encountered edge cases
3. **Regulatory volatility**: requirements based on legislation that changes frequently (EU directives, administrative rules) → higher freshness check priority than treaty-based requirements (more stable)
4. **Contradiction risk**: requirements that involve thresholds (deadlines, quotas, fees) → higher priority for exact verification than categorical requirements

Combining these signals in a Bayesian active learning framework gives ReloPass a principled, adaptive queue for lawyer verification that minimizes the time to bring all requirements to the desired accuracy level.

---

**Closing Synthesis**

CS329H reframes ReloPass's core challenge — maintaining a highly accurate, human-preferred knowledge graph of immigration requirements — as a preference learning problem with clear theoretical foundations and practical algorithms. The key insight is that the lawyer sign-off pipeline IS an RLHF system: it generates pairwise preference signals that can be used to train reward models, drive active learning selection, and calibrate requirement quality scores. The mechanisms, models, and algorithms covered in CS329H (Bradley-Terry, Thompson sampling, active bisection, Constitutional AI, incentive compatibility design) provide the formal vocabulary to make this pipeline more efficient, more accurate, and more trustworthy — ensuring that Case Command surfaces not just technically correct requirements, but requirements that HR generalists and immigration lawyers actually prefer and trust.

The deepest lesson from CS329H for ReloPass: preferences are not ground truth. They are noisy, strategic, context-dependent, and sometimes self-contradictory signals from humans who are doing their best under constraints of time, knowledge, and incentive. Building a system that learns stably and usefully from these signals requires the full toolkit of this course: rigorous choice models, efficient elicitation algorithms, incentive-compatible mechanisms, ethical frameworks for aggregation, and HCI-grounded interface design for the humans providing the signal.
